<meta>16757 written in pencil in top left margin.</meta>

# Barnacles [or Clack Geese] p. liii, liv.

In Ireland there are many Fowls, which are called Bernacæ,
which are produced after a wonderfull manner, contrary to y^e common
course of Nature, somewhat like wild Geese, but of a lesser size: they
first appear like Gum, upon logs of fir, w^ch are tossed upon y^e
surface of y^e Sea: after this they are covered in & shut up by y^e
Sea Weed & small shells sticking together upon y^e Wood, where having
room enough to be compleatly formed, they hang by their bills,
till being in due time compleatly featherd, they either drop
down into y^e Water, or else immediately fly off into y^e open air.
They are supposed to receive their nutriment & growth after
a most secret & wonderfull manner from y^e sap of y^e Firr, & y^e
Salt Water. I my self have often seen with my own eyes, above
a thousand of these little birdlings, perfectly shapen & enclosed
with shells, upon y^e Sea shore, hanging together from one piece of
Wood. They never produce any Eggs like other birds; they are never
hatched after y^e common manner. They are never observed to
tread, or to have any nests, in any part of y^e World. From hence it
is, that in some parts of Ireland the Bishops & other Religious
make no scruple of eating them on Fast Days as not beeing Flesh,
because not produced by flesh—

<vocab>Barnacles, Clack Geese, Bernacæ, Ireland, birdlings, Fast Days</vocab>

# Autalops //

<image-desc>A framed manuscript miniature within a red and blue border. It depicts an antelope (the Autalops) with long, serrated horns that have become entangled in the branches of a green shrub. To the left, a hunter wearing a grey tunic and red hose approaches, raising a large broad-axe with both hands. He also appears to carry a curved horn or tool in his left hand. The background consists of horizontal bands of blue and pale beige. The animal has red streaks on its flank, possibly representing blood or stylized fur patterns.</image-desc>

Est animal quod dicitur autalops acerrimū nimis. ita ut nullus uenator ei possit appropinquare. Habet autē longa cornua serre figā. ita ut possit resecare arboreſ altaſ & magnaſ & ad terrā psternere. Cum autē sitierit uadit ad flumen magnū eufraten. Est autē ibi frutex qui grece dr̄ heriane habens uirgulta subtilia & plixa & incipit ludere cornu ad herianā & dum ludit obligat cornua in uirgultis. Audiens uenator uocē eius. uenit & occidit eum. Sic & tu homo qui studes sobrius & castus ee. & spiritaliter uiuere cuius duo cornua sunt duo testamenta p que poteris resecare & eradere omnia uicia corporalia & spiritalia. Caue ab ebrietate ne obligaris luxuria & uoluptate. & interficiaris a diabolo. Vinum enim & mulieres apostatare faciunt homineſ a deo.

## Gripes.

<image-desc>A manuscript illumination of a griffin (Gripes) contained within a double-lined frame of red and orange. The griffin is a hybrid creature with the front part of an eagle (dark grey feathers, hooked beak, and large spread wings) and the hindquarters of a lion (reddish-brown fur and a long tail). It is depicted in profile, lunging forward to bite the back of a grey horse-like animal. The horse is pinned to the ground on its back with its legs kicking upward in a struggle. The background inside the frame is split between a deep blue at the top and a faded pink/tan at the bottom.</image-desc>

<header>Castor.</header>

<image-desc>A circular miniature (tondo) with a thick red border illustrates the medieval legend of the beaver. On the left, two hunters stand; one wears a blue tunic and holds a sword and a spear. A third man in a pink tunic reaches toward the animals. In the foreground, a large grey-brown beaver with a broad, scaly tail is depicted biting its own testicles—a behavior described in bestiaries as a defense mechanism to escape hunters. Above it, another beaver is shown from behind while a hunter holds the severed organs. The scene is set against a plain parchment background.</image-desc>

<margin>hyena</margin>
Est animal quod dicitur hyena in sepulchris mortuorum habi-
tans. eorumque corpora uescens. Cuius natura est. ut aliquando
masculus sit aliquando femina & ideo. est. immundum animal.
Qui cum spina riget. continua unitate flecti nequit. nisi toto
corpore circumactu. Solum multa mira de ea refert. primum quod se-
quitur stabula pastorum & circuit domos pro nocte. & assiduo au-
ditu addiscat uocamen. quod exprimere possit imitatione uocis
humane. ut in hominem astu accitum nocte seuiret. Vomitusque
humanos mentiatur. falsisque singultibus sic sollicitatos canes deuo-
rat. Qui forte si uenantes umbram eiusdem dum sequuntur contigerint. la-
trare nequeunt uoce perdita. Eadem hyena confractione corporum sepultos
busta eruit. Huic assimilantur filii israel. qui ab initio deo uiuo ser-
uierunt. Postea diuitiis & luxurie dediti. idola coluerunt. ideo
propheta comparauit synagogam immundo animali dicens. Facta est mihi he-
reditas mea quasi spelunca hyene. Quicunque igitur inter nos luxurie
& auaricie inseruiunt. huic belue comparantur. cum nec uiri nec fe-
mine sint. id est nec fideles si perfidi sint. sine dubio de quibus ait sa-
lomon. Vir duplex animo. inconstans est in omnibus uiis suis. De qui-
bus ait dominus. Non potestis deo seruire & mammone. Hec belua la-
pidem in oculis tenet nomine hyenam que siquis sub lingua tenuerit. fu-
tura predice creditur. Verum hyena quodcumque animal ter lustrauerit moue-
re se non potest. Quapropter magicam scientiam inesse ei pronuntiauerunt. In
ethiopie parte coit cum leena. unde natus monstrum. cui corocote nomen
est. Voces hominum & ipsa pro effectat. Nunquam commutatione cibum sibi
mouent sine mutatione comedunt. In ore gingiua nulla. Dent-
unus atque perpetuus qui ut nunquam retundatur. natalis capsula-
rum modo clauditur.

<meta>image quality: excellent. Features a medieval Bestiary entry for a bear. Includes a later pencil annotation "A Bear" above the illumination.</meta>

<image-desc>
A large, colorful illumination set within a triple-arched architectural frame with red columns and blue backgrounds. Inside the frame, a large brown bear with a shaggy, textured coat is depicted in profile, hunched over and licking a small, shapeless red mass on the ground. This illustrates the medieval belief that bear cubs were born unformed and had to be "licked into shape" by their mothers. The frame is topped with two turret-like structures with red roofs and gold finials.

### Onager.

<image-desc>A rectangular framed miniature with a red and green geometric border. It depicts an onager (wild ass), rendered in reddish-brown and white. The animal is shown in a violent act, biting the testicles of a smaller, younger onager that lies on its back beneath it. This scene illustrates the medieval belief mentioned in the text that male onagers castrate their male offspring to prevent them from breeding.</image-desc>

<image-desc>A framed miniature painting with a red outer border and a green inner background. It depicts four badgers (taxus) engaged in an activity related to their burrows. One badger lies on its back inside a large, circular, golden-colored hole or mound filled with swirling patterns, clutching a wooden bar with its paws. Three other badgers are positioned outside the hole; two of them appear to be pulling or holding the wooden bar, while the third stands at the bottom. This illustrates the medieval legend of badgers using one of their own as a "wagon" to transport earth out of their burrows.</image-desc>

# Aquila.

<image-desc>An illuminated miniature from a medieval bestiary framed within a decorative border of red, orange, and white bands. A dark sun with stylized red rays is positioned at the top center of the frame. The interior background of the frame features a blue diapered pattern of small quatrefoils. The image depicts three eagles: one stands at the top right with wings partly raised; another dives head-first toward green wavy mounds at the bottom; a third eagle on the left is shown in profile holding a fish in its talons. The miniature is followed by a paragraph of text beginning with a large decorative initial 'A' in blue and red filigree.</image-desc>

->**Aurifrigius.**<-

<image-desc>A rectangular illustration framed by a thick red border depicting three ospreys (aurifrigii) interacting with a body of water. The birds are rendered in shades of grey and brown with detailed plumage. The background is a pale peach color, decorated with small red and white six-pointed star motifs. At the bottom, stylized green wavy lines represent water containing three visible fish. The bird on the left is diving downwards with wings spread, clutching a fish in its talons. The central bird is flying away from the viewer with its back and wings exposed. The bird on the right stands or floats on the water's surface with its wings partially extended. The scene is likely a depiction of the osprey's hunting behavior as described in medieval bestiaries.</image-desc>

## Alcion

Alcion maritima auis est que in litoribz fetus suos edere solet ita ut arenis oua sua deponat . medio se hyemis . Ha id tēporis fouendi habet deputatū partibz . quando maxime insurgit mare . tūc utiqz uehementior fluctus illiditur quo magis repentine placiditatis sollempnitate huius auis eluceat grā . Namqz ubi undosum fuerit mare positis ouis subito mitescit & omnes cadunt uento&z pcelle . flatusqz aurarū mitescunt . ac placidum uentis stat mare . donec oua foueat alcion sua . Septē autē dief fouet sunt . quibuf dief sit eductio pullo&z . fetusqz absolutio . Illico aliof quoqz septem adiungit dies quibz enutriat partus suof donec incipiant adolescere . Hoc mireris tam exiguū nutrimenti tēpus cum absolutio fetuum tam pauco&z sit dierū . Tamē auis grā miracula diuinitatis indultam habet ut hos .xiiij. dies nautae psumpte serenitatis obseruent quos & alcionidas uocant quibz nullus mot' pcellose tēpestatis horrescat .

accipiendo id est a capiendo nomen sumpsit. Est autem avis rapiendi aliis avibus avida. Ideo vocatur accipiter hoc est raptor. Unde et paulus dicit <margin>2. Cor. 11.</margin> Sustinetis enim si quis accipit. Fertur autem accipiter circa pullos suos impius esse. nam dum viderit eos posse temptare volatus. nullas eis prebet escas. sed verberat pennis. et a nido papitat atque a tenero compellit ad predam. ne adulta pigrescant. Cavet ne in tenera etate pigrescant. ne solvantur deliciis ne marcescant ocio. ne discant cibum magis expectare quam querere. ne nature sue deponant vigorem: intermittunt studium nutriendi. ut usu rapiendi audere compellant. Accipiter sanctum virum significat. ut pote rapit regnum dei. de quo in iob scriptum est. <margin>Job. 39.</margin> Numquid in sapientia tua plumescit accipiter expandens alas suas ad austrum. id est: nunquid cuilibet electo tu intelligentiam contulisti ut flante spiritu sancto cogitationum alas expandat quatenus pondera vetuste consuetudinis abiciat. et virtutum plumas in usum novi volatus sumat. Potest etiam per hunc accipitrem renovata gentilitas designari. *Qualiter accipiter plumescere debet.*

<image-desc>
A large, detailed medieval illustration framed by a thick red border. It depicts various aquatic creatures and sea monsters characteristic of a Bestiary:
1. Centrally, a large blue-grey hybrid creature with the forequarters of a horse (head, neck with mane, and front legs) and a long, scaly fish tail with multiple fins.
2. Above the horse-fish is a long, slender grey eel or fish.
3. To the right are several smaller fish of various species.
4. Below the horse-fish, a red serpentine creature with a dragon-like head and pointed ears is curled in the center. 
5. In the bottom left, a red sea-dog or sea-monster with a long, curled tail.
6. To its right, a small blue-grey quadrupedal creature with a tail.
7. In the bottom right corner, a large grey-scaled fish is shown with its mouth open, swallowing a smaller green fish.
The creatures are drawn with dark outlines and flat color washes on the natural parchment background.
</image-desc>

One man steers with a long oar while others work at the mast and sail. Two small, pale, nude figures are perched atop the rigging. Below the ship, a massive, scaly green fish swims. Arbores maiores umbrose frondibus. Lucus est densitas arborum
alta uocata. Hoc nomine. eo quod excludat solem & in sublime
surgat. Inscitio dicitur cum in alieno trunco surculus. sedem arbo
ris sterilis inseritur. Plante sunt de arboribus. Plantaria uero
que ex semine nata sunt cum radicibus & a terra propria transferuntur.
Radix appellatur. quod quasi radiis quibusdam fixa terris in profunda
ingratur. Nam phisici dicunt parē esse altitudinem radi
cum & arborum. Truncus est statura arboris in sistentia
dicta. Corticem ueteres corucem uocabant. dictus autem conger
quod corio lignum tegat. Liber est corticis pars interior. dictus a
liberato cortice idest ablato. Est enim medium quiddā inter lignū
& corticem. Rami sunt qui de trunco manant. sicut a ramis
surculi. Surculi sunt a passione terre nuncupati. Virgul
tum. est quod de radice pullulat. Ramus de ipso robore arbo
ris. Virga que de ramis. Proprie autem uitulum appellatur
quod ad radicem arboris nascitur: & quasi in utile ab agricolis
amputatur. & hinc dictum uitulum. quod ex uirga tollitur. Virga
uel a uirute dicitur. quod uim in se habeat multam uel a uiriditate
uel quia parit indicium ē quod uireat. Unde hac utuntur ma

& nuntii. & legati utuntur. Flagella dicuntur summe arbo
rum partes: ab eo quod crebros uentos sustinere sciant. Qui
comae uocantur qui comas folia. grece hyle dicuntur. Unde
